People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, I just... I don't feel like myself anymore. It's hard to explain, but it's like the person I used to be is just gone. I mean, I used to do so much. I was always outdoors, hiking up the trails every weekend, you know? I loved the fresh air, the physical challenge of it. And I was coaching the local youth soccer team, which took up a lot of my time, but I really enjoyed it. I even played guitar, just for myself mostly, but sometimes with a few buddies. Now? I haven't picked up the guitar in months. The strings are probably rusted. I stopped coaching, I stopped hiking. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone will ring, and I'll just look at it, knowing it's my brother or a friend, and I just let it go to voicemail. I don't know why. And then some days, a lot of days actually, I just sit in my small rental house. Just sit there for hours. I know there are things I need to do—dishes, laundry, paying bills, even just getting up to get a glass of water—but it's like there's this heavy weight on me, and I just can't make myself start. I'll sit in the chair and think about getting up, but my body just won't do it. It's like the starter motor is broken. I just sit and watch the light change in the room as the day goes by, and I can't do anything about it. It's just... I don't know what's wrong with me.

I haven't picked up the guitar in months. The strings are probably rusted. I stopped coaching, I stopped hiking. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone will ring, and I'll just look at it, knowing it's my brother or a friend, and I just let it go to voicemail. I don't know why. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, never. This is... this is completely new territory for me, and I think that's part of what's so frustrating about the whole thing. I mean, I'm 45 years old. I've got my bachelor's degree, I've spent my whole career in protective services—working security details, managing access, dealing with the public, handling emergencies. It's a job that demands you to be switched on, you know? You have to be alert, you have to be ready to move and make decisions at a moment's notice. And I was good at it. I was always the guy who could handle the double shifts, the night rotations. 

Sure, you get tired. Back in, I don't know, maybe five or six years ago, we were incredibly short-staffed and I was pulling sixty-hour weeks for months on end. I was exhausted. I'd come home and my feet would be absolutely killing me, and I'd just collapse on the couch. But that was normal tired. A good night's sleep, and I still got up the next day, put my uniform on, and did what I had to do. Even when life got really hard—like when I had a bad breakup in my thirties, or when things got stressful with my family—I never lost my drive. I always bounced back. I'd play some music, go for a long hike up in the hills, shake it off, and get back to normal. 

But this thing happening now? It's not like being tired from work or being bummed out about life. It's like my battery has been completely removed. I have never in my entire life just sat in a chair and watched the shadows move across the floorboards all afternoon because I couldn't figure out how to make my brain tell my legs to stand up. I've always been a doer. So, no, nothing like this has ever happened to me before. Not once. It's like I woke up a few months ago and somebody had swapped me out with a broken version of myself, and I just keep waiting to snap out of it, but I don't.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32, F33, F31, F34.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you into the clinic today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I just... I don't feel like myself anymore. It's hard to explain, but it's like the person I used to be is just gone. I mean, I used to do so much. I was always outdoors, hiking up the trails every weekend, you know? I loved the fresh air, the physical challenge of it. And I was coaching the local youth soccer team, which took up a lot of my time, but I really enjoyed it. I even played guitar, just for myself mostly, but sometimes with a few buddies. Now? I haven't picked up the guitar in months. The strings are probably rusted. I stopped coaching, I stopped hiking. I don't even call people back anymore. My phone will ring, and I'll just look at it, knowing it's my brother or a friend, and I just let it go to voicemail. I don't know why. And then some days, a lot of days actually, I just sit in my small rental house. Just sit there for hours. I know there are things I need to do—dishes, laundry, paying bills, even just getting up to get a glass of water—but it's like there's this heavy weight on me, and I just can't make myself start. I'll sit in the chair and think about getting up, but my body just won't do it. It's like the starter motor is broken. I just sit and watch the light change in the room as the day goes by, and I can't do anything about it. It's just... I don't know what's wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before in your life, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. This is... this is completely new territory for me, and I think that's part of what's so frustrating about the whole thing. I mean, I'm 45 years old. I've got my bachelor's degree, I've spent my whole career in protective services—working security details, managing access, dealing with the public, handling emergencies. It's a job that demands you to be switched on, you know? You have to be alert, you have to be ready to move and make decisions at a moment's notice. And I was good at it. I was always the guy who could handle the double shifts, the night rotations. \n\nSure, you get tired. Back in, I don't know, maybe five or six years ago, we were incredibly short-staffed and I was pulling sixty-hour weeks for months on end. I was exhausted. I'd come home and my feet would be absolutely killing me, and I'd just collapse on the couch. But that was normal tired. A good night's sleep, and I still got up the next day, put my uniform on, and did what I had to do. Even when life got really hard—like when I had a bad breakup in my thirties, or when things got stressful with my family—I never lost my drive. I always bounced back. I'd play some music, go for a long hike up in the hills, shake it off, and get back to normal. \n\nBut this thing happening now? It's not like being tired from work or being bummed out about life. It's like my battery has been completely removed. I have never in my entire life just sat in a chair and watched the shadows move across the floorboards all afternoon because I couldn't figure out how to make my brain tell my legs to stand up. I've always been a doer. So, no, nothing like this has ever happened to me before. Not once. It's like I woke up a few months ago and somebody had swapped me out with a broken version of myself, and I just keep waiting to snap out of it, but I don't.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... I mean, if you really want to trace it all back, things have been unraveling since I moved into this smaller rental house. That was about a year and two months ago, maybe a little more. It was right after my divorce was finalized. 

You know, the divorce itself was... well, it was a long time coming. We had been arguing for years, just constantly bickering about money, about my hours at work, about everything under the sun. By the time we actually signed the papers, I thought I was going to feel relieved, you know? Like this huge burden was lifted off my shoulders. And for a minute, I think I did. But then I had to pack up all my stuff—which wasn't much, to be honest, mostly just my clothes, my guitar, some camping gear—and move into this tiny little place. 

It's so different from the house we had. That place had a big yard, a garage where I could tinker with things, lots of light. This rental... it's cramped. The windows are small, the neighbors are loud, and the walls are so thin you can hear every car that drives by. I remember the first week I was there, I just sat on the edge of the mattress—I didn't even have a bed frame yet, just the mattress on the floor—and I looked at the boxes stacked up everywhere, and it just hit me. This was my life now. Forty-five years old, starting over in a place that smells like old carpet and bleach. 

And I think that's when it started, really. Not all at once, not like a light switch flipping off, but just this slow fade. At first, I just didn't want to unpack. I told myself I was too tired from work, that I'd get to it on the weekend. But then the weekend would come, and I'd just look at the boxes and feel... nothing. No motivation, no desire to make the place look nice. And then it bled into the rest of my life. The hiking stopped, the guitar got shoved in a closet. So, yeah, it was the divorce and the move. Fourteen months ago. That's when the wheels started coming off the wagon, and I just haven't been able to put them back on since. It just keeps getting worse, and I don't know how to stop it.

You mean like... am I sad? Like, feeling down in the dumps or crying all the time? Honestly, no. It's not really like that. 

I mean, I know it probably sounds like I should be depressed, right? Given everything I just said about the divorce, the crappy apartment, the boxes sitting everywhere... My sister actually called me a few weeks ago—well, she left a voicemail because I didn't pick up the phone, like I told you—but she said on the machine, "Are you depressed? You sound depressed." And I just sat there in my chair, listening to her voice echo out of the speaker into the room, and I really thought about it. But I don't feel sad. 

I know what sadness feels like. When my dad passed away back in '08, now that was sad. I remember sitting in my truck after the funeral, and I just bawled for an hour. My chest physically ached, my throat was so tight I could barely breathe, and I felt like the whole world was just crushing down on me. That's what being depressed or grieving feels like to me. You feel it in your bones. 

But this thing right now? It's completely different. I don't cry at all. I haven't shed a single tear since the day I moved into this rental. I don't feel that heaviness in my chest, I don't feel miserable or sorry for myself. It's more like... it's just blank. Like someone took a giant eraser to my brain and wiped out all the feelings. I can sit in that chair and have the TV on, and a comedy could be playing, or the news could be showing some terrible tragedy, and it all just washes over me exactly the same. No highs, no lows. Just a flat line. I don't feel hopeless, I just feel kind of hollowed out, like the inside of me is just empty space. So if you're asking if I'm sitting around weeping or feeling heartbroken all day... no, I'm really not. I just don't feel much of anything at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My sleep? Honestly, it's... it's probably the only thing that's exactly the same as it's always been. I know people who go through hard times and they can't sleep, or they sleep all day long, but that's not me right now. 

Like I was telling you about that mattress on the floor—I did eventually get a frame for it, just a cheap metal one from a discount store down the street, but it squeaks if I move even an inch. You'd think that would keep me up, but it really doesn't. I usually just go lay down around ten o'clock or so. I don't even really have a bedtime routine anymore, I don't read or anything, I just kind of migrate from the chair in the living room to the bed when it gets late enough. And once I'm there, I just close my eyes and I'm out. 

I don't toss and turn. I don't lay there with my mind racing about the divorce or work or the boxes sitting in the corner. Because like I said, it's just blank up there. There's no worry keeping me awake, no anxiety buzzing around in my head. So I sleep straight through the night. Seven, maybe eight hours, just like I did when I was working the heavy security rotations. And then morning comes, and I wake up around six, usually right before the sun really comes up. 

The actual sleeping part is perfectly fine, completely normal. It's just that once I wake up, I just lay there. I can lay there for hours, just staring at the popcorn ceiling, listening to the neighbor's dog bark or the cars going by, because I can't find a single reason to actually put my feet on the floor and start the day. But the sleep itself? No, no problems there at all. I get my regular hours in and that's about it.

Appetite... no. No, I wouldn't say so. I mean, I still eat. It's not like I'm wasting away or anything. 

I have this routine, see. Even though everything else feels completely stripped down, I still go to the grocery store every Tuesday. I go to the smaller one over on 5th Street, not the big mega-mart across town, because the parking lot is easier and I just don't want to deal with the crowds. And I buy the exact same things every single time. Two loaves of bread, a pack of sliced turkey, a carton of eggs, and a stack of those frozen dinners—usually the Salisbury steak or the chicken alfredo ones, because they're cheap, you know, and they only take four minutes in the microwave. 

So I'm eating the same amount I always have. I don't really get cravings anymore, I don't sit around thinking about going out for a big juicy steak or anything like I used to, but when my stomach actually starts growling, I just go to the kitchen and put something in it. It's purely mechanical at this point. I'll just stand in front of the open fridge... sometimes I'll just stare at the shelves for a good five or ten minutes, just looking at the mustard bottle and the milk jug, before I finally just grab a slice of cheese or throw a frozen tray in the microwave. 

And as for my weight, it hasn't budged. I wear these heavy-duty tactical pants for my security job, size 34 waist, and they fit exactly the same as they did a year ago. My work belt goes to the exact same notch, the third hole from the end. The leather's actually all worn down right at that specific spot because I've used it for so many years, so I'd definitely notice if I had to move it up or down a notch. I stepped on the scale at the pharmacy a few weeks back while I was waiting for a prescription, and it said 185. That's my normal walking-around weight, has been since I was in my thirties. So, no, I'm not starving myself, and I'm not stress-eating either. It's just... steady. Like I said, I just eat to keep the engine running, even if I'm not really going anywhere. No changes there at all.

My work belt goes to the exact same notch, the third hole from the end. The leather's actually all worn down right at that specific spot because I've used it for so many years, so I'd definitely notice if I had to move it up or down a notch. I stepped on the scale at the pharmacy a few weeks back while I was waiting for a prescription, and it said 185. That's my normal walking-around weight, has been since I was in my thirties. So, no, I'm not starving myself, and I'm not stress-eating either. It's just... steady. Like I said, I just eat to keep the engine running, even if I'm not really going anywhere. No changes there at all.\n\n[Last Question]\n```json\n{\n  \"intent\": \"Assess for feelings of worthlessness or excessive guilt to evaluate diagnostic criteria for a major depressive episode.\",\n  \"question\": \"Have you been\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Or like I'm worthless? No... no, I wouldn't say that at all. 

I mean, I know a lot of guys go through a divorce and they really beat themselves up over it. They sit around and talk about how they ruined everything, or how they're a complete failure. My ex-wife, when she was finally packing up to leave, she actually stood right there in the front hallway of our old place—the house with those nice oak floors I spent three whole weekends sanding and refinishing by hand back in, I think it was 2019—and she screamed at me that I was a brick wall. She said I ruined the marriage because I just stopped caring about anything. 

And you know, I guess maybe someone else would have internalized that. They'd sit around in their new, crappy apartment feeling like a piece of garbage, replaying every mistake they ever made, feeling this massive weight of guilt for not trying harder or not being a better husband. But I just watched her pack those big blue hard-shell suitcases—the ones we bought for a trip to the coast that we never even ended up taking—and I didn't feel a single drop of guilt. I didn't feel like a bad person. I just watched her load up the back of her car and drive away, and it was just... neutral. 

And as for feeling worthless... no, I don't have that either. I know I can still do things. I show up to my security shifts, I walk the perimeter, I check the padlocks on the south gate, I log the delivery license plates exactly the way the supervisor's manual says to. I know I'm doing the job perfectly fine. I don't sit in the guard shack at 2 AM thinking I'm a waste of space or some kind of burden to the world. It's not that I think I have no value... it's just that the value doesn't seem to mean anything anymore. 

It's sort of like... well, imagine you have a perfectly good generator sitting out in the garage. It's not worthless, the engine is fine, it could work if it needed to, but if you never put gas in it and never actually turn it on, who cares what it's worth? It's just sitting there taking up space. 

So no, I don't sit here hating myself or feeling guilty about how things turned out. Honestly, hating yourself seems like it would take a lot of energy. You have to really care about yourself to be able to hate yourself, if that makes any sense. And like I was trying to explain earlier, I just don't have those kinds of feelings buzzing around in my head anymore. I'm just here. So no, no guilt, and I don't feel worthless at all.

Thoughts of death? Or like... wanting to kill myself? No. No, I don't have any thoughts like that. I know that's probably the next logical question when someone is living the way I am right now, but no. 

It really just goes back to what I was saying about the energy it takes to feel things. To want to die, I think you have to be in a lot of pain. You have to be hurting so bad that you just want it to stop, or you have to be so angry at the world that you just want to leave it behind. But I don't have that pain, and I'm definitely not angry. I'm just... blank. 

I mean, think about my job. I work the night shift security at the industrial park over on the east side, right by the old rail yard. Every night around 3:15 AM, this massive freight train comes through, hauling coal and lumber up north. It shakes the whole guard shack, rattles the windows in their frames. I have to go out and stand by the crossing gate to make sure none of the delivery trucks try to drive around it before the arms come up. I stand so close I can feel the wind hitting my face, and the noise is completely deafening. A person who wanted to die, they might look at that train and think about stepping out onto the tracks. They might feel some kind of pull. But me? I just watch the wheels go by. I just count the cars because I have to log the crossing time in the ledger with my blue pen. It's just a train. It doesn't mean anything else to me.

And at home, you know, I told you about those frozen dinners I eat. I have this heavy-duty box cutter, the kind with the retractable razor blade and the bright orange handle that I bought at the hardware store a few years ago, and I use it to slice up the cardboard boxes so they fit into the recycling bin. The city gave us these new blue bins last year and they're actually a bit narrower than the old green ones, so you really have to break things down flat or the lid won't close, and if the lid isn't closed the automated truck arm can't grab it right. Anyway, I hold that blade every single Tuesday night. And I just look at it, and it's just a tool to cut cardboard. I don't look at it and think about my wrists or anything like that. The thought just doesn't even form. 

I'm not looking for an exit, doctor. I'm not really looking for anything at all. I'm just waking up, going to the guard shack, coming home, and laying on that mattress. There's no urge to end it, because there's no strong feeling about being alive in the first place. It's just a flat line. So no, I'm completely safe in that regard. I don't think about death at all.

I know there are things I need to do—dishes, laundry, paying bills, even just getting up to get a glass of water—but it's like there's this heavy weight on me, and I just can't make myself start. I'll sit in the chair and think about getting up, but my body just won't do it. It's like the starter motor is broken. I just sit and watch the light change in the room as the day goes by, and I can't do anything about it. It's just... I don't know what's wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this before in your life, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. This is... this is completely new territory for me, and I think that's part of what's so frustrating about the whole thing. I mean, I'm 45 years old. I've got my bachelor's degree, I've spent my whole career in protective services—working security details, managing access, dealing with the public, handling emergencies. It's a job that demands you to be switched on, you know? You have to be alert, you have to be ready to move and make decisions at a moment's notice. And I was good at it. I was always the guy who could handle the double shifts, the night rotations. \n\nSure, you get tired. Back in, I don't know, maybe five or six years ago, we were incredibly short-staffed and I was pulling sixty-hour weeks for months on end. I was exhausted. I'd come home and my feet would be absolutely killing me, and I'd just collapse on the couch. But that was normal tired. A good night's sleep, and I still got up the next day, put my uniform on, and did what I had to do. Even when life got really hard—like when I had a bad breakup in my thirties, or when things got stressful with my family—I never lost my drive. I always bounced back. I'd play some music, go for a long hike up in the hills, shake it off, and get back to normal. \n\nBut this thing happening now? It's not like being tired from work or being bummed out about life. It's like my battery has been completely removed. I have never in my entire life just sat in a chair and watched the shadows move across the floorboards all afternoon because I couldn't figure out how to make my brain tell my legs to stand up. I've always been a doer. So, no, nothing like this has ever happened to me before. Not once. It's like I woke up a few months ago and somebody had swapped me out with a broken version of myself, and I just keep waiting to snap out of it, but I don't.\n\nDoctor: Did any specific stressful life event, loss, or major change happen around the time you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I mean, if you really want to trace it all back, things have been unraveling since I moved into this smaller rental house. That was about a year and two months ago, maybe a little more. It was right after my divorce was finalized. \n\nYou know, the divorce itself was... well, it was a long time coming. We had been arguing for years, just constantly bickering about money, about my hours at work, about everything under the sun. By the time we actually signed the papers, I thought I was going to feel relieved, you know? Like this huge burden was lifted off my shoulders. And for a minute, I think I did. But then I had to pack up all my stuff—which wasn't much, to be honest, mostly just my clothes, my guitar, some camping gear—and move into this tiny little place. \n\nIt's so different from the house we had. That place had a big yard, a garage where I could tinker with things, lots of light. This rental... it's cramped. The windows are small, the neighbors are loud, and the walls are so thin you can hear every car that drives by. I remember the first week I was there, I just sat on the edge of the mattress—I didn't even have a bed frame yet, just the mattress on the floor—and I looked at the boxes stacked up everywhere, and it just hit me. This was my life now. Forty-five years old, starting over in a place that smells like old carpet and bleach. \n\nAnd I think that's when it started, really. Not all at once, not like a light switch flipping off, but just this slow fade. At first, I just didn't want to unpack. I told myself I was too tired from work, that I'd get to it on the weekend. But then the weekend would come, and I'd just look at the boxes and feel... nothing. No motivation, no desire to make the place look nice. And then it bled into the rest of my life. The hiking stopped, the guitar got shoved in a closet. So, yeah, it was the divorce and the move. Fourteen months ago. Concentration? Or focusing on things, making decisions, that kind of thing? No, I wouldn't say I have any trouble with that. Actually, my focus is probably exactly the same as it's always been. Maybe even a little sharper, honestly, just because there's no... no background noise in my head anymore, if you know what I mean. 

Take my job at the industrial park. I sit in that guard shack, and there's this bank of six monitors on the desk. They upgraded them last November to these flat-screen LED ones, 1080p, so the picture is really crisp compared to the old CRT monitors that used to have that weird green tint and those rolling static lines every time a truck drove by. Anyway, I have to watch camera four, which points at the loading dock for the logistics company, and camera two, which covers the employee parking lot. I can sit there and watch the dust motes float across the screen in the halogen floodlights for three hours straight. I don't lose my train of thought. I don't drift off. If a stray cat walks behind the wooden pallets at 2:14 AM, I see it. I log it in the book. My focus is perfectly fine. I don't get distracted because there's nothing pulling my attention away.

And as for decisions... well, making a decision usually implies you have a preference, right? That you're trying to figure out what's best, or what will make you the happiest, or what you want the most. But since I don't really care about the outcome either way, deciding is actually incredibly easy. It's just a flow chart in my head. If it's Tuesday, I go to the small grocery store. If it's raining, I wear the yellow slicker with the silver reflective tape that they keep in the locker room, locker number four. If the supervisor calls and asks me to take an extra shift on Sunday, I just look at the schedule, see that I have nothing else to do, and I say yes. 

There's no agonizing over anything. I don't sit around weighing the pros and cons or getting confused about what to do next. The supervisor's manual says to check the south gate padlocks at 4:00 AM, so at 3:55 AM, I put on my boots, I grab my heavy flashlight—it's a black Maglite, takes four D batteries, weighs about three pounds so it doubles as a baton if you really need it to—and I walk out the door and do it. It's just... execution. You see a requirement, and you meet it. You don't have to think very hard about it. So, no, my concentration is fine, and decisions are just automatic. No problems there at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
That I'm moving slower? Like my body is heavy or something? No... no, definitely not slow. If anything, it’s the exact opposite of that. People have noticed things, I guess, but not that I’m sluggish. 

Actually, on my patrols... especially when I have to cover the main logistics office building instead of the perimeter. Usually this guy Dave covers the inside, but he has this terrible sciatica that flares up whenever the barometric pressure drops, so they pull me in from the guard shack to walk the interior floors. Anyway, when I'm in there, I’ve actually started reacting before I can even slow myself down. 

It’s just this instant reflex. I’ll be walking down the main corridor, the one right past the breakroom with that really tight-knit gray commercial carpet, and I’ll just suddenly drop and crouch behind the reception counters. Just completely duck down into cover without even deciding to do it. And if any of the late-shift inventory staff happen to be walking by with their clipboards, I’ll immediately throw my hand out and signal them to get back, to keep them behind the corners. 

And if they speak to me—like if they stop and ask, "Hey, what are you doing?"—I find myself repeating their last words right back to them. "Doing... doing." I have to say it back to check if they're part of it. You can tell a lot by how the words sound when you bounce them back, it helps you figure out if they're on the inside of what's happening or just oblivious. 

And then I’ll pace. I get locked into this one specific track. From the edge of the reception desk to the glass double doors by the elevators, exactly eighteen paces, and I just pace that exact same route over and over and over again. I don't even realize how intense it is until I finally look up and see that the logistics staff have backed themselves flat against the drywall and they look genuinely scared of me. 

So, no, I'm not moving in slow motion. I'm reacting way before I can hit the brakes, and it's definitely making the office staff nervous. But nobody has ever told me I look slowed down.

What's going on... well, when I say 'part of it,' I mean part of the whole operation. The network. I know it sounds extreme when I just say it out loud in a doctor's office like this, but for months now, I've been certain—I mean absolutely, one hundred percent certain—that the guys across from my rental and some of the people at work are working together. 

I rent this little half-duplex over on Elm Street. It has a chain-link fence, and I try to keep the ivy growing thick on it so nobody can see in from the street, but it doesn't matter, because the guys in the house directly across from me, they sit on their porch and they watch. And they aren't just watching, they're tracking my patrol routes. They know exactly when I leave for the industrial park, and they know exactly where I walk when I'm there. I'll even try to throw them off, right? Like, instead of taking the perimeter fence line at 1:00 AM, I'll cut through the loading dock, but they already know. 

And they've been testing my locks, too. I have a Schlage deadbolt on my front door—it's a heavy-duty one, solid brass—and every time I lock it before I leave for my shift, I make sure the keyhole is perfectly vertical. Exactly ninety degrees. But I'll come home at 7:00 in the morning, and it's tilted. Just a fraction of a millimeter, but it's tilted. They're testing the tumblers while I'm gone to see how much resistance it has. 

And the reason I know the people at work are in on it with the guys across the street is because the details keep lining up too perfectly to just be some kind of random coincidence. It's the cars and the comments. There's this dark blue sedan, maybe a late 2000s model, missing the front left hubcap. I'll see it parked two houses down from my rental when I leave at 10:30 PM. And then, at 3:15 AM, that exact same car with the missing hubcap is idling by the south gate of the industrial park. Just sitting there with the parking lights on. 

And then the inventory staff on the late shift, the ones I was telling you about... they make these little comments. They'll walk by and say things like, "Hope you're staying on your track tonight," or "Sure is a locked-down neighborhood." You don't just use those specific words. You don't say "track" or "locked-down" unless you are the one monitoring the track and testing the locks. It's impossible for that to be random. It's statistically impossible. So when I duck down, or when I repeat their words back to them, I'm testing them. I'm listening to the micro-tremors in their voice to see if they're the ones feeding my coordinates back to the guys on Elm Street. Because they're all connected, doctor. I just haven't figured out exactly what they're planning to do once they finally get through that deadbolt.
